Unsteady Navier-Stokes Equations On A Stationary Surface
Charles M. Elliott, Achilleas Mavrakis

Abstract
. In this paper we consider a fully discrete numerical method for the unsteady Navier-Stokes equations on a smooth closed stationary surface in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . We use the surface finite element method (SFEM) with a generalized Taylor-Hood finite element pair 𝐏 k u \mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}} / P k p ​ r \mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}} / P k λ \mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}} , where we enforce the tangential condition of the velocity field weakly, by introducing an extra Lagrange multiplier λ \lambda . Depending on the richness of the finite element space involving this extra Lagrange multiplier we present a full discrete stability and error analysis. For the velocity, we establish optimal L 2 ​ ( a h ) L^{2}(a_{h}) -norm bounds ( a h a_{h} - an energy norm) when k λ = k u k_{\lambda}=k_{u} and suboptimal with respect to the geometric approximation error when k λ = k u − 1 k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1 (optimal when super-parametric finite elements are used). For the pressure, optimal L 2 ​ ( L 2 ) L^{2}(L^{2}) -norm error bounds are established when k λ = k u k_{\lambda}=k_{u} . Assuming further regularity assumptions for our continuous problem, we are also able to show optimal convergence (using super-parametric finite elements again) when k λ = k u − 1 k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1 . Numerical simulations that confirm the established theory are provided, along with a comparative analysis against a penalty approach.



1.
Introduction

In mathematical modeling, fluid equations on surfaces emerge naturally as ways to describe biological phenomena, like lipid membranes, material science in foams and biophysics, see
[
1
,
44
,
46
]
. For a stationary surface
\Gamma
the
(unsteady) generalised surface Navier-Stokes
problem is to: Find the velocity field
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t):\Gamma\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, surface pressure
p(\cdot,t):\Gamma\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\int_{\Gamma}pds=0
and Lagrange multiplier
\lambda(\cdot,t):\Gamma\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that solves on
\Gamma

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}}+{\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{u}}-2\mu\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(E({\mathbf{u}}))+\nabla_{\Gamma}p+{\mathbf{u}}+\lambda{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}={\bm{f}},\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}=0,\\
{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=0,\end{cases}

for all
t\in[0,T]
, where
\mu
is the viscosity coefficient,
{\bm{f}}\in(L^{2}(\Gamma))^{3}
a given force field, and

\lambda
a normal force field to be determined. Essentially we view
\lambda
as a Lagrange multiplier for the constraint that the normal component of
{\mathbf{u}}
is zero. Such models have been presented and studied in
[
26
]
,
[
17
]
for the
steady Stoke
case. Here the rate-of-strain (deformation) tensor
E(\cdot)

[
35
]
, is defined for an arbitrary vector field
{\mathbf{v}}
, by

(1.2)

\displaystyle E({\mathbf{v}})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}).

where
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}:={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}
(with adjoint
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov,t}{\mathbf{v}}=(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}})^{t}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
) and
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
denotes the projection to the tangent space of
\Gamma
.

Similarly, considering
{\mathbf{u}}\equiv{\mathbf{u}}_{T}+({\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}_{T}:={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{u}}
, we may consider the
(unsteady) tangential surface Navier-Stokes
problem, that is : Find the velocity field
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t):\Gamma\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, surface pressure
p(\cdot,t):\Gamma\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\int_{\Gamma}pds=0
that solves on
\Gamma
that solves on
\Gamma

(1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}}_{T}+{\mathbf{u}}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{u}}_{T}-2\mu{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(E({\mathbf{u}}_{T}))+\nabla_{\Gamma}p+{\mathbf{u}}_{T}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\bm{f}},\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}_{T}=0.\end{cases}

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Similar systems have been established and studied in
[
41
,
8
]
and references therein.

In this work, we consider a finite element discretization of (
1.1
) using
Taylor-Hood
surface finite elements. Surface finite elements methods (SFEM) have been studied extensively in the literature; see
[
15
,
18
,
14
]
for elliptic and parabolic problems on a stationary and evolving surfaces
\Gamma
. There has also been extentions to vector or tensor-valued functions for elliptic equations
[
22
,
23
]
. Recently, there have been numerous studies around surface Stokes problems
[
13
,
3
,
43
,
17
]
and Navier-Stokes
[
32
,
33
,
44
,
19
]

on evolving and stationary surfaces applying the surface finite element methods.

Discretizations of fluid problems on surfaces pose a lot of difficulties, such as the approximation of covariant derivatives, geometric quantities, e.g. mean curvature, and the imposition of the tangential condition on the velocity field. There has been a plethora of papers and techniques dealing with such problems, each with its own advantages and disadvantages. The tangential surface Stokes problem has been studied using TraceFem in
[
27
,
28
,
40
]
, which uses a background bulk mesh, not fitted to the surface.
Moreover, surface finite element methods have been applied and analyzed, with different formulations, various finite element spaces, and techniques. The main difficulty in all of them is enforcing the tangential constraint in the discrete space. This would not lead to
H^{1}
-conforming finite elements, due to the discontinuity across the edges of the triangulated mesh. One approach that has been employed to address this issue is using the stream function formulation
[
4
]
, where the tangentiality condition is satisfied exactly.
This approach is
H^{1}
-conforming, however, it becomes necessary to compute various geometric quantities. Regarding approximations of solutions in the velocity-pressure formulation, approaches that enforce the tangentiality condition strongly, has also been considered. In
[
3
]
, an
H(\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma})
conforming finite element method with an interior penalty approach to enforce
H^{1}-
continuity weakly is analysed, and in
[
13
]
an
H(\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma})
conforming finite element method is constructed with the help of surface Piola transforms w.r.t. the set of faces of each vertex. These methods are quite complicated to implement numerically. Simpler
H^{1}
conforming
Taylor-Hood
finite elements for the velocity-pressure formulation have also been studied, where one relaxes the tangential constraint via a penalty parameter (penalty formulations)
[
43
,
22
,
24
]

(also see
[
27
,
28
,
40
]
for the TraceFEM case)
or via a Lagrange multiplier (Lagrange formulation)
[
17
,
30
]
. Regarding the penalty formulations, to obtain optimal convergence we see that approximations of a new higher-order normal, and geometric quantities, mainly the mean curvature, are required. Furthermore, a correct choice of the penalty parameter
\eta
is also important; see
[
24
,
43
,
22
]
. On the other hand, for the Lagrange formulation no penalty term arises, but one has to consider the choice of approximation of the extra Lagrange multiplier, as it plays a role in both the stability of the system and the optimal convergence; see
[
17
]
. Depending on the choice; same order as the velocity approximation or one less, one either establishes optimal convergence (for the full velocity) but with worse conditioning of the underlying system of equations or suboptimal with respect to the geometric approximation error, therefore
super-parametric surface finite elements
have to be used (mainly for the tangential velocity). We follow this approach.

1.1.
Main results

In this paper, we analyze a fully discrete
H^{1}
conforming
Taylor-Hood
surface finite element method
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}}
/
\mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}}
/
\mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}
for the time-dependent surface Navier-Stokes (
1.1
), where the tangential constraint is enforced via an extra Lagrange multiplier
\lambda_{h}
. By,
k_{u}
,
k_{pr}
,
k_{\lambda}
we represent the degree of polynomials used for the velocity field
{\mathbf{u}}
, pressure
p
and extra Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
approximations, respectively. For inf-sup stability reasons we always consider
k_{u}=k_{pr}+1
, see
Lemmas

6.2
and
6.3
, while we also consider two options in terms of the choice of approximation of
\lambda
,
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
and
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
. The time-dependent surface Navier-Stokes was studied in
[
40
]
, where the authors used a TraceFEM
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-\mathrm{P}_{1}
Taylor-Hood penalty formulation, with pressure stabilization and assumed exact integration over
\Gamma
.
In our case, we consider a
k_{g}
-order approximation of the surface,
\Gamma_{h}
, and thus we deal with geometric errors arising from this approximation. That means we have to treat the approximated terms carefully and specifically the inertia term. Moreover, we add the zeroth-order term
{\mathbf{u}}
in (
1.1
) only so that we can ease some calculations. It is not difficult to see that even without this term the results that will be established still hold (despite this see also
Remark

7.5
where this zeroth-order term might be important in certain cases).

In this paper, we analyze the error between the continuous primitive variables of (
3.6
) and the discrete ones arising from the discrete weak Lagrange formulation (
UWL
), where we first prove stability and eventually error bounds. In the main result
Theorem

7.7
we prove

L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}
and
L^{2}_{a_{h}}
, where
a_{h}
a discrete energy norm (
5.8
), velocity error bounds for both choices of
k_{\lambda}
. This is established in the case of low regularity assumption; see
1
, with the help of a new modified surface Ritz-Stokes map (
5.39
) which helps us isolate velocity errors from pressure errors. We notice that for the
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
case, due to a limiting geometric error
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{k_{g}-1})
; see (
7.35
), one has to further assume that
k_{g}\geq 2
and use
super-parametric surface finite elements
to obtain optimal convergence.
However, this is not necessary once we choose
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
, as is evident in (
7.34
), which we note also holds in the case of
planar triangulation
, i.e.
k_{g}=1
. See also the numerical results in
Section

8
and specifically
Section

8.2
for a more detailed discussion along with an example.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxed{\tiny\text{Regularity of solutions}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxed{\tiny\text{Low}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxed{\tiny\text{High}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxed{\tiny\text{Velocity Error Estimate}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxed{\tiny\text{Pressures Error Estimates}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxed{\tiny\text{Pressures Error Estimates}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta t\leq ch}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta t\leq ch}

The pressure bounds, on the other hand, need a bit more consideration in regards to the regularity assumptions, the choice of the approximation of the extra Lagrange multiplier and the
inf-sup
condition used. This stems from the fact that to find pressure bounds one needs to deal with the discretization of the time derivative
(\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n}-\mathbf{u}_{h}^{n-1})/\Delta t
, and in turn bound it in an appropriate norm. Considering
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
, and low regularity assumptions for continuous solutions, we can derive optimal
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{H_{h}^{-1}}
error (stability) pressure estimates, as presented in our main pressure result
Theorem

7.10
(
H_{h}^{-1}
a dual norm (
5.3
)). These bounds are established with the help of a discrete inverse Stokes operator (
6.6
), the dual energy norm estimate
Lemma

6.5
and an
L^{2}\times H_{h}^{-1}
discrete
inf-sup
condition (
6.5
), therefore only establishing error bound for
\lambda_{h}
in this weaker dual norm.
On the other hand, if further regularity assumptions are made for the velocity; see
2
, we notice that a
W^{1,\infty}
-bound for the standard Ritz-Stokes map (
5.40
) and the discrete integration by parts formula (
5.25
) enables us to prove convergence results when
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
, as presented in
Theorem

7.17
; see
Remark

7.5
for more details .In this case using the
L^{2}\times L^{2}

inf-sup
condition (
6.4
) instead, we prove, once again, suboptimal results w.r.t. the geometric approximation error, hence the importance of employing
super-parametric finite elements
for optimal convergence.

1.2.
Outline

In
Section

2
we introduce our notation along with our continuous function spaces. The continuous variational formulation of (
1.1
) is briefly introduced in
Section

3
, while the temporal and space discretizations are discussed in the next
Section

4
, where we also recall some basic results involving surface lifting, geometric errors and basic interpolation estimates. In
Section

5
we define the finite element spaces and bilinear forms that will be used throughout, and introduce new discrete surface Ritz-Stokes and Leray projections that are necessary leading up to the stability and a-priori error bounds. In that section, lastly, we present our fully discrete variational formulation (
UWL
). In
Section

6
we present the velocity and pressure stability results. The error analysis is discussed in
Section

7
. In the first part, we consider
low regularity assumptions
and establish velocity error estimates independent of the choice of approximation of the extra Lagrange multiplier
k_{\lambda}
. Then, we prove error bounds for the pressure that hinge on the choice of
k_{\lambda}
. Namely, first for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
with
low regularity assumptions
, we prove optimal error pressure bounds in an
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{H_{h}^{-1}}
-norm and second for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
where
additional regularity assumptions
are considered, in the
L^{2}_{L^{2}}\times L^{2}_{L^{2}}
-norm. Finally, numerical results are presented in
Section

8
, which support the theoretical results.

2.
Notation and Differential Geometry

2.1.
The closed smooth surface

We will consider our surface
\Gamma
to be a closed, oriented, compact
C^{m}
two-dimensional hypersurface embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for some fixed
m
. In
[
17
]
it was required to assume that
m\geq 4
. This assumption arises due to technical results related to the proof of the discrete inf-sup stability. We also require this condition here for similar reasons. Note that since
\Gamma
is the boundary of an open set we choose the orientation by setting
{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
to be the unit outward pointing normal to
\Gamma
. Let
d(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the signed distance function, defined in
[
15
]
, and
for
\delta>0
, let
U_{\delta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the tubular neighborhood
U_{\delta}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ :|d(x)|<\delta\}
. Then we may define the closest point projection mapping to
\Gamma
as
\pi(x)=x-d(x){\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\pi(x))\in\Gamma
for each
x\in U_{\delta}
for
\delta>0
small enough, see
[
20
,
15
]
. Moreover,
\nabla d(x)={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(\pi(x))
for
x\in U_{\delta}
. We can see that
d(\cdot)
and
\pi(\cdot)
are of class
C^{m}
and
C^{m-1}
on
\overline{U_{\delta}}
.

The extended normal allows us to define the extended Weingarten map on the surface and in
U_{\delta}
, by
\bm{H}:=\nabla{\mathbf{n}}^{e}=\nabla^{2}d
, which is of class
C^{m-2}
. The mean curvature of
\Gamma
is
{\bm{\kappa}}:=tr(\bm{H})
. We also define the orthogonal projection operator onto the tangent plane
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}(x)={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(x)\otimes{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}(x)
for
x\in\Gamma
that satisfies
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}^{T}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}^{2}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
, and
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=0
.

Using the projection
\pi
we may extend functions
{\mathbf{v}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
on
\Gamma
to the tubular neighbourhood
U
, by

(2.1)

{\mathbf{v}}^{e}(x)={\mathbf{v}}(\pi(x)),\ \ \ x\in U_{\delta}.

This extension is constant in the normal direction of
\Gamma
and thus contains certain useful additional properties in comparison to other regular extensions. The extension of
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
to the
\delta-
strip
U_{\delta}
, is defined as

(2.2)

{\mathbf{P}}={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{e}(x)\otimes{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{e}(x)={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}}(x)\otimes{\mathbf{n}}(x),\ \text{ for }x\in U_{\delta},

where we have used
{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}^{e}={\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}\circ\pi={\mathbf{n}}
. We can then easily see that
{\mathbf{P}}|_{\Gamma}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
.

2.2.
Scalar functions and vector fields

We start by defining useful quantities for
scalar
functions following
[
15
]
. For a function
f\in C^{1}(\Gamma)
we define the
tangential (surface) derivative
of
f
as

(2.3)

\nabla_{\Gamma}f(x)={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}(x)\nabla f^{e}(x),\ \ \text{with }\ \underline{D}_{i}f(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{3}P_{ij}\partial_{j}f^{e}(x),\ \ \ x\in\Gamma,

where
(\nabla f^{e})_{i}=\partial_{i}(f^{e})
,
1\leq i\leq 3
is the Euclidean derivative and
\nabla_{\Gamma}f(x)=(\underline{D}_{1}f(x),

\underline{D}_{2}f(x),

\underline{D}_{3}f(x))^{t}
is a column vector.
As noted in
[
15
, Lemma 2.4]
the definition of the tangential gradient is independent of the extension.

We now introduce derivatives of surface
vector fields
as in
[
19
,
35
,
17
]
. We begin by defining tangential derivatives, analogous to the scalar case, as Euclidean derivatives of extended quantities (this time-vector fields) and proceed to establish the covariant derivatives.

Definition 2.1

(Tangential derivative)
.

Let
{\mathbf{v}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{T}
be a smooth vector field, and
{\mathbf{v}}^{e}
the smooth extension in (
2.1
), the tangential gradient of this vector field is defined by

(2.4)

(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}})_{ij}=(\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})_{ij}=\sum_{l=1}^{3}\partial_{l}v^{e}_{i}P_{jl},

where
(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}})_{ij}=\underline{D}_{j}v_{i}
. This derivative is expressed as a
3\times 3
matrix, which may be presented as

(2.5)

\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\\
v_{3}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\underline{D}_{1}v_{1}&\underline{D}_{2}v_{1}&\underline{D}_{3}v_{1}\\
\underline{D}_{1}v_{2}&\underline{D}_{2}v_{2}&\underline{D}_{3}v_{2}\\
\underline{D}_{1}v_{3}&\underline{D}_{2}v_{3}&\underline{D}_{3}v_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

We note again that due to the extension (
2.1
),
\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}|_{\Gamma}
holds. With the help of the tangential derivatives for vector fields we may define the Weingarten map
\bm{H}
on the
\delta-
strip
U_{\delta}
again (see
[
15
,
20
]
)

(2.6)

\bm{H}:=\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}},\ \text{ with }\ \bm{H}{\mathbf{n}}=0,\ \ \ \bm{H}{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}\bm{H}=\bm{H},

where we observe that it is tangent to
\Gamma
. Note that
\bm{H}
is of class
C^{m-2}
and thus bounded on
\Gamma
.

Definition 2.2

(Covariant derivatives)
.

Let
{\mathbf{v}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{T}
be a smooth vector field, and
{\mathbf{v}}^{e}
the smooth extension in (
2.1
), the covariant derivative
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}
is then defined by

(2.7)

\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}|_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}=\{P_{ik}\partial_{l}v^{e}_{k}P_{lj}\}_{ij}

where the derivative can be expressed as a
3\times 3
matrix.

One has to distinguish these two different gradient operators. Notice that the covariant derivative, compared to the tangential derivative defined in (
2.4
), is a tangential tensor field. Using now the Einstein summations we see that the surface divergence of a vector function
{\mathbf{v}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is given by

(2.8)

\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{v}})=tr(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}})=tr({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})=P_{ik}\partial_{k}v_{l}^{e}P_{li}=tr(\nabla{\mathbf{v}}^{e}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}})=tr(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}).

One can always split the a vector field
{\mathbf{v}}
into a tangent and a normal component
i.e.

{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}_{T}+{\mathbf{v}}_{n}
with
{\mathbf{v}}_{T}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{v}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{n}=({\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})\,{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
, where also the following useful formulae holds

(2.9)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}+\bm{H}v_{n}.

We also define with the help of (
2.8
) the surface divergence of a tensor function
{\mathbf{F}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
:

(2.10)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}):=\begin{bmatrix}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}_{1,j})\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}_{2,j})\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{F}}_{3,j})\end{bmatrix},\quad j=1,2,3.

By
({\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{v}}
we denote the tangent vector

(2.11)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{v}}=v_{j}P_{jk}\partial_{k}v_{l}^{e}P_{li}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{v}}_{T}.

Finally, we present the following integration by parts rule for smooth enough
{\mathbf{v}}
and
\xi
:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi d\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}\xi\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma}{\bm{\kappa}}\xi({\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}).

2.3.
Function spaces

We now define function spaces on the surface
\Gamma
as in
[
15
]
. By
L^{p}(\Gamma)
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, we define the function space that consists of functions
\xi:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that are measurable w.r.t. the surface measure, with the standard
L^{p}
-norms,
i.e.

||\cdot||_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}
. By
(\cdot,\cdot)_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}
we denote the usual
L^{2}(\Gamma)
inner product.

Also
H^{k}(\Gamma)
denotes the standard Sobolev spaces also presented in
[
15
]
for
\xi:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with the corresponding norm being given by

(2.13)

\displaystyle||\xi||_{H^{k}(\Gamma)}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}||\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}\xi||^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)},

where
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}
denote all the weak tangential derivative of order
j
, i.e.
\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}\xi=\underbrace{\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\cdot\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}}_{\text{ j times}}\xi
. One can easily extend in the case vector-valued functions pointwise. More specifically for a vector fields
{\mathbf{v}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
the space
\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)=(H^{1}(\Gamma))^{3}
is equipped with the following norm

(2.14)

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{v}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}^{2}=||{\mathbf{v}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+||\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{v}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}.

We set

L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma):=\{\xi\in L^{2}(\Gamma)|\ \int_{\Gamma}\xi\,d\sigma=0\},

equipped with the standard
L^{2}
-norm and the subspace of tangential vector fields:

\bm{H}^{1}_{T}:=\{{\mathbf{v}}\in H^{1}(\Gamma)^{3}\>|\>{\mathbf{v}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=0\}

endowed with the
H^{1}
-norm (
2.14
). We also consider the subspace of
weakly tangential divergence-free
vectors

(2.15)

{\mathbf{V}}^{div}=\{{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\,:\,b^{L}({\mathbf{v}},\{q,\xi\})=0\text{ for all }\{q,\xi\}\in L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma)\}.

3.
Variational formulation

3.1.
Preliminaries and Bilinear forms

Definition 3.1
.

For
{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
and
\{q,\lambda\}\in L_{0}^{2}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma)
we define the following

(3.1)

\displaystyle a({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma}E({\mathbf{u}}):E({\mathbf{v}})+{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,

(3.2)

\displaystyle c({\mathbf{u}};{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}){\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{u}})\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\,d\sigma,

(3.3)

\displaystyle b^{L}({\mathbf{u}},\{q,\lambda\})

\displaystyle:=-\int_{\Gamma}q\ \textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}_{T}\,d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma}\lambda u_{n}\,d\sigma.

The following inequality, known as
surface Korn’s inequality
, was established in
[
26
]
. There exists a constant
c_{K}>0
such that

(3.4)

c_{K}||{\mathbf{w}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}\leq||E({\mathbf{w}})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+||{\mathbf{w}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}~~\text{ for all }{\mathbf{w}}\in\bm{H}^{1}_{T}.

In the case where
{\mathbf{w}}\in\bm{H}^{1}_{T}
we also define the energy norm

(3.5)

||{\mathbf{w}}||_{a}=||E({\mathbf{w}})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+||{\mathbf{w}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}.

We present the following Helmholtz-Leray decomposition, which will be useful later on in order to prove a stability estimate in a discrete dual energy norm; see
Lemma

6.5
in
Section

6
.

Lemma 3.1

(Helmholtz-Leray decomposition)
.

For every
{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
there exists unique
\phi\in H^{2}(\Gamma)
,
{\mathbf{v}}_{n}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
and
\Pi^{div}({\mathbf{v}})\in{\mathbf{V}}^{div}
such that

{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}_{n}+\Pi^{div}({\mathbf{v}})+\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi.

See Appendix
A
for a proof of this decomposition.

3.2.
Lagrange multiplier variational formulations

Consider (
1.1
), by applying integration by parts we look for a solution of the following variational problem:

(LP)
: Given
{\bm{f}}\in(L^{2}(\Gamma))^{3}
, determine
{\mathbf{u}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\Gamma))\cap L^{2}([0,T];\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma))
, with
\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}}\in L^{2}([0,T];\bm{H}^{-1}(\Gamma))
and
\{p,\lambda\}\in(L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma))\times L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Gamma)))
satisfying
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0)={\mathbf{u}}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Gamma)
on
\Gamma
such that,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\langle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Gamma),H^{1}(\Gamma)}+a({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +c({\mathbf{u}};{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})\ +\!\!\!\!&b^{L}({\mathbf{v}},\{p,\lambda\})=({\bm{f}},{\mathbf{v}})_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma),\\
&b^{L}({\mathbf{u}},\{q,\xi\})=0\ \ \ \text{ for all }\{q,\xi\}\in(L_{0}^{2}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma)),\end{cases}

and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where
{\mathbf{u}}_{0}
satisfies
b^{L}({\mathbf{u}}_{0},\{q,\xi\})=0

\forall\,\{q,\xi\}\in(L_{0}^{2}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma))
. The uniqueness and well-posedness of the above system follow similarly to the Stokes case
[
17
]
. In our case, the existence of the second system (
1.3
) has been proved in
[
41
]
, from which we can see the uniqueness of our system, since they have the same solutions. We also have the following
inf-sup
condition result presented in
[
17
]
.

Lemma 3.2

(Inf-sup Condition)
.

Let
\Gamma\in C^{2}
and compact. Then there exists a constant
c_{b}
such that,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\inf_{\{p,\lambda\}\in(L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma)\times H^{-1}(\Gamma))}\ \sup_{{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)}\frac{\tilde{b}({\mathbf{v}},\{p,\lambda\})}{||{\mathbf{v}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||\{p,\lambda\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)\times H^{-1}(\Gamma)}}\geq c_{b}>0,

Considering the above lemma and following standard Faedo-Galerkin techniques, well-posedness can be established. We omit further details, but one can see that the following a-priori estimates hold; see also
[
40
]
,

(3.8)

\displaystyle||\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{-1}(\Gamma))}

\displaystyle+||{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\Gamma))}+||{\mathbf{u}}||_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{1}(\Gamma))}

\displaystyle+||\{p,\lambda\}||_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Gamma))}\leq||{\mathbf{u}}_{0}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+||{\bm{f}}||_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Gamma))}.

4.
Preliminaries

In this section we begin by presenting our temporal discretization scheme (based on the backward Euler time discretization), along with some basic results involving the space discretization, mainly geometric errors arising from the approximation of the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
, surface lifting to
\Gamma
etc., cf.
[
15
,
18
]
. Finally, we introduce some known interpolants and their estimates.

4.1.
Time-stepping scheme

For the time discretization we split the time interval
I=[0,T]
uniformly into
N
time intervals
I_{n}=[t_{n-1},t_{n}]
with nodes
t_{n}=n\Delta t
,
n=0,1,...,N
and uniform time step size
\Delta t=t_{n}-t_{n-1}=T/N
. We approximate the first derivative as followed

(4.1)

\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}}=\frac{{\mathbf{u}}^{n}-{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}}{\Delta t},

where
{\mathbf{u}}^{n}={\mathbf{u}}(t^{n})
, which corresponds to a
BDF1
type time discretization. So the semi-discrete variational formulation of (
3.6
) reads as:

For
n=0,1,...\,,N
find
{\mathbf{u}}^{n}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
,
\{p^{n},\lambda^{n}\}\in L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma)
, given
{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
,
\{p^{n-1},\lambda^{n-1}\}\in L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma)
, such that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\frac{{\mathbf{u}}^{n}-{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}}{\Delta t},{\mathbf{v}})_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+a({\mathbf{u}}^{n},{\mathbf{v}})\ +c({\mathbf{u}}^{n-1};{\mathbf{u}}^{n},{\mathbf{v}})\ +\!\!\!\!&b^{L}({\mathbf{v}},\{p^{n},\lambda^{n}\})=({\bm{f}}^{n},{\mathbf{v}})_{L^{2}(\Gamma)},\\
&b^{L}({\mathbf{u}}^{n},\{q,\xi\})=0,\end{cases}

for all
{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
and
\{q,\xi\}\in L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma)
.
Notice that since we use
BDF1
time discretization we have linearized the trilinear form in the following sense, by replacing
{\mathbf{u}}^{n}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{u}}^{n}
with
{\mathbf{u}}^{n-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{u}}^{n}
.

4.2.
Space Discretization

4.2.1.
Triangulated surfaces

We now want to discretize our surface
\Gamma
. We construct the discrete triangulated surface
\Gamma_{h}
.
But first, one approximates
\Gamma
by a polyhedral approximation
\Gamma^{(1)}_{h}
contained in the tubular neighborhood
U_{\delta}
i.e.
\Gamma^{(1)}_{h}\subset U_{\delta}
, whose vertices lie on
\Gamma
and which is equipped with an admissible and conforming subdivision of triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}^{(1)}
so that
\Gamma^{(1)}_{h}=\cup_{\tilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{(1)}}\tilde{T}
, see also
[
18
, Section 6.2]
.

We work with polynomial Lagrange finite elements, thus point evaluations (on each vertex) act as our degrees of freedom. So, for
k_{g}\geq 2
we consider
\{\phi_{1},...,\phi_{n_{k_{g}}}\}
be the Lagrange basis functions of degree
k_{g}
associated to a set of nodal points
\{\tilde{a}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n_{k_{g}}}
of a triangle
\tilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{(1)}.
Then with the help of the
k_{g}-
th order Lagrange interpolant
[
15
]
we define the higher-order geometry

\displaystyle\pi_{k_{g}}(\tilde{x})=\mathcal{I}_{h}^{k_{g}}\pi(\tilde{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k_{g}}}\pi(\tilde{a}_{j})\phi_{i}(\tilde{x}),\qquad\text{ for }\tilde{x}\in\tilde{T}.

Applying the above on each element
\tilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{(1)}
yields the
admissible
and
conforming
high-order triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}
, written as
\mathcal{T}_{h}=\cup\{F_{T}(\tilde{T})\ |\ \tilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{(1)}\}
and thus generates the high-order surface approximation

\Gamma_{h}=\bigcup_{\tilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\{F_{T}(\tilde{x})\ |\ \tilde{x}\in\tilde{T}\}.

We denote by
h_{T}
the diameter of a simplex
T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
and set
h:=\text{max}\{\text{diam}(T)\,:\,T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\}
. We assume the subdivision
\mathcal{T}_{h}
is shape-regular hence the following inequality holds

(4.3)

c_{1}h_{T_{i}}\leq h_{T}\leq c_{2}h_{T_{i}},\quad\text{for all }T_{i}\in\omega_{T}.

for fixed constants
c_{1},\,c_{2}
, where
\omega_{T}
denotes the number of neighboring elements to
T
assumed to be bounded by a fixed constant; see
[
7
]
. We further assume that the family of triangulation is also
quasi-uniform

[
18
, Definition 6.22]
, hence associated with a quasi-uniform constant
\sigma
such that

(4.4)

{\max_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}h_{T}}/{\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}h_{T}}\leq\sigma.

We now denote element-wise outward unit normal to
\Gamma_{h}
by
{\mathbf{n}}_{h}
and define the discrete projection
{\mathbf{P}}_{h}
onto the tangent space of
\Gamma_{h}
by

{\mathbf{P}_{h}}(x)={\bm{I}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h}(x)\otimes{\mathbf{n}}_{h}(x),\quad x\in T,\text{ where }T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}.

Also, the corresponding discrete Weingarten map is
\bm{H}_{h}:=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}.
As such, one can define the discrete tangential surface operators
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}},\,\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}
.
For the curved triangulation, we may also consider the set of all the edges of the triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}
, denoted by
\mathcal{E}_{h}
.
For each edge, we define the outward pointing unit co-normals
{\mathbf{m}}_{h}^{\pm}
with respect to the two adjacent triangles
T^{\pm}
. Notice that on discrete surfaces in general

[{\mathbf{m}}_{h}]|_{E}={\mathbf{m}}_{h}^{+}+{\mathbf{m}}_{h}^{-}\neq 0.

For more details on the construction of the triangulated surface, see
[
17
,
18
]
and references therein.

4.2.2.
Geometric approximation errors

The approximation of a smooth surface
\Gamma
by a discrete triangulated surface
\Gamma_{h}
of order
k_{g}
introduces geometric errors. Here we present some known key geometric estimates, see
[
30
,
38
]
. Define
{\mathbf{P}},{\mathbf{n}}
the extension of the
{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}},\,{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}
to the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
.

Lemma 4.1

(Geometric Errors)
.

For
\Gamma_{h}
and
\Gamma
as above, we have the following estimates

(4.5)

\displaystyle||d||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{n}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||\bm{H}-\bm{H}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}-1},

\displaystyle||[{\mathbf{m}}_{h}]||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||{\mathbf{P}}[{\mathbf{m}}_{h}]||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{h})}\leq ch^{2k_{g}}.

and thus we may derive the approximations,

(4.6)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}_{h}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||{\mathbf{P}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\quad||1-{\mathbf{n}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}}.\quad

It also follows that

(4.7)

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}},\text{ and }\qquad||{\mathbf{P}}{\mathbf{P}_{h}}{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}.

All the above constants
c
are independent of the mesh parameter
h
.

Proof.

The estimates in the first line of (
4.5
) are already known; see
[
15
,
11
]
. The results in the second line are shown in
[
38
]
. The rest can be easily computed as a consequence of these first results, see
[
18
,
11
]
and references therein.
∎

Next, let
\mu_{h}(x)
be the Jacobian of the transformation
\pi(x)|_{\Gamma_{h}}
:
\Gamma_{h}\rightarrow\Gamma
such that
\mu_{h}(x)d\sigma_{h}=d\sigma
, then the following estimate holds (see
[
11
]
)

(4.8)

\displaystyle||1-\mu_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1},

from which we get the uniform bounds
||\mu_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq c
and
||\mu_{h}^{-1}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq c
.

4.2.3.
Finite Element Function Spaces

Since certain quantities on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
, e.g. projections and their derivatives, are only defined elementwise on each surface finite element
T
, it is convenient to introduce broken surface Sobolev spaces, with their respected norm
[
18
]
. The norm
||\cdot||_{H^{m}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}
related to the
broken Sobolev space

\bm{H}^{m}(\mathcal{T}_{h})=(H^{m}(\mathcal{T}_{h}))^{3}
is defined by

(4.9)

\displaystyle||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{m}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}^{2}=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||^{2}_{H^{m}(T)},

for a vector field
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{m}(\Gamma_{h})
. The same notation holds in the case of scalar quantities.

Given a triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}
of
\Gamma_{h}
, as described above, we define the
H^{1}-
conforming Lagrange finite element space on
\Gamma_{h}
, with finite elements of degree
k\geq 0
:

(4.10)

S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}:=\{v_{h}\in C^{0}(\Gamma_{h}):v_{h}|_{T}=\hat{v}_{h}\circ\hat{F}_{T}^{-1}\text{ for some }\hat{v}_{h}\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\hat{T}),\ \text{ for all }T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\},

where
\mathbb{P}^{k}
is the space of piecewise polynomials on the reference element of degree
k
and
\hat{F}_{T}
is some reference map to some reference element; see
[
18
,
17
]
for more details. Due to the mapping
\hat{F}_{T}^{-1}
we observe that the space does not necessarily consists of polynomials over each triangle
T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, unless we consider affine finite elements
[
18
, Example 6.7]
, consisting of piecewise linear triangulation and first-order polynomial functions. Note as well that
S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}\subset C^{0}(\Gamma_{h})\cap H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
.

All the above can be easily expanded for vector valued functions where now the finite element space takes the form:

(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k})^{3}=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}\times S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}\times S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}\subset\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h}).

We denote the velocity approximation order by
k_{u}
, and for the pressures
p
and
\lambda
, we represent their approximation orders as
k_{pr}
and
k_{\lambda}
, respectively. Writing

\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}}:=(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{u}})^{3},\ \mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}}:=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{pr}},\ \mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}:=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{\lambda}}

we use the notation
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}}
–
\mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}
to denote the generalized
Taylor-Hood
surface finite elements, where
k_{pr}=k_{u}-1
,
k_{\lambda}\geq 1
.

4.2.4.
Lifting to the exact surface

We want to relate quantities on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
and
\Gamma
, since our finite element solutions live on
\Gamma_{h}
and the continuous solutions on
\Gamma
. For that we use an extension/lifting procedure; see also
[
15
,
11
,
18
]
.

We already assumed that
h
is small enough such that
\Gamma_{h}\subset U_{\delta}
, hence the projection
\pi(\cdot)
is well-defined and a bijection. Therefore, for every triangle in the triangulation
T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
there exists an induced curvilinear triangle
T^{\ell}=\pi(T)\subset\Gamma
by the triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}
of
\Gamma_{h}
. We may then define the conforming quasi-uniform triangulation, see
[
18
]
,
\mathcal{T}_{h}^{\ell}
of
\Gamma
as

\Gamma=\cup_{T^{\ell}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{\ell}}T^{\ell}.

We define the
lift
operator on
\Gamma_{h}
of any finite element function
v_{h}:\Gamma_{h}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with the help of the closest point projection operator by

(4.11)

v_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x)):=v_{h}(x),\quad\text{for }x\in\Gamma_{h}.

Similar to the extension (
2.1
), we also define the inverse lift operator for a function
v:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
by,

(4.12)

v^{-\ell}(x):=v(\pi(x)),\quad\text{for }x\in\Gamma_{h}.

Notice that the two extensions (
2.1
) and (
4.12
) agree on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
i.e.
v^{-\ell}=v^{e}|_{\Gamma_{h}}
since we assumed
\Gamma_{h}\subset U_{\delta}
.
Lastly, we also introduce the space of lifted finite element functions given by

(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k})^{\ell}=\{u_{h}^{\ell}\in H^{1}(\Gamma):u_{h}\in S_{h,k_{g}}^{k}\}\subset H^{1}(\Gamma).

We now compare functions defined on the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
and the exact surface
\Gamma
. We relate their tangential derivatives with the help of the lift extension defined above. Set
{\bm{B}_{h}}={\mathbf{P}_{h}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
.

Scalar Functions ::

Using the extension of a scalar function
v_{h}:\Gamma_{h}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, (
4.11
) and the chain rule we have that the discrete tangential gradient of
v_{h}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
for
x\in\Gamma_{h}
is

(4.13)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}v_{h}(x)={\mathbf{P}_{h}}\nabla v_{h}(x)={\mathbf{P}_{h}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla v_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))={\bm{B}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma}v_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x)),

We also notice that
{\bm{B}_{h}}:T_{x}(\Gamma_{h})\mapsto T_{\pi(x)}(\Gamma)
is invertible; see
Lemma

4.2
, i.e. we have that the map
{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}
on the tangent space
T(\Gamma)
is given by

{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}|_{T_{x}\Gamma}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H})^{-1}({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}}){\mathbf{P}_{h}},

thus we have for
u_{h}:\Gamma_{h}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and for
x\in\Gamma_{h}
that (see
[
12
]
),

(4.14)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}v_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}({\bm{I}}-d\bm{H})^{-1}({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}})\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}v_{h}(x)={\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}v_{h}(x).

Noticing that the matrix
{\mathbf{G}}=({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}})=({\bm{I}}-\frac{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\otimes{\mathbf{n}}}{{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}}){\mathbf{P}_{h}}
we can easily calculate that
{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}{\bm{B}_{h}}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
and
{\bm{B}_{h}}{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}={\mathbf{P}_{h}}
.

Vector Valued Functions ::

Considering the tangential gradients of vector valued function (
2.4
) and (
2.5
), we may apply the lift extension component-wise, for each row, and with similar calculations to the scalar case we may get, after factoring out the common map
{\bm{B}_{h}}
, that

(4.15)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}(x)=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))=\nabla\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x)){\mathbf{P}_{h}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x)){\bm{B}_{h}}^{t}.

Likewise for the inverse transformation we get

(4.16)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}(\pi(x))=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}(x)({\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1})^{t}.

Recalling the results from
[
25
,
15
,
22
,
17
]
we have the following lemma, which also shows that
{\mathbf{B}}_{h}
is invertible.

Lemma 4.2

(Estimates for
{\mathbf{B}}_{h}
)
.

For sufficiently small
h
we have the following bounds

(4.17)

\displaystyle||{\bm{B}_{h}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq c,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad||{\bm{B}_{h}}^{-1}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq 1,

(4.18)

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}-{\bm{B}_{h}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}},\qquad\qquad||{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}-{\bm{B}_{h}}^{t}{\bm{B}_{h}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}.

4.2.5.
Norm equivalence

We use
\sim
to denote the norm equivalence independent of
h
and by the estimates in
Lemma

4.1
we recall the following Lemma, involving equivalence of norms on different surfaces.

Lemma 4.3

(Norm Equivalence)
.

Let
v_{h}\in H^{j}(T),

j\geq 2
for
T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
a face of
\Gamma_{h}
and let

T^{\ell}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}^{\ell}
be a face of the lifted surface
\Gamma
. Then for
h
small enough we have that following equivalences

(4.19)

\displaystyle||v_{h}^{\ell}||_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}

\displaystyle\sim||v_{h}||_{L^{2}(T)}\sim||\tilde{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\tilde{T})},

\displaystyle||\nabla_{\Gamma}v_{h}^{\ell}||_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}

\displaystyle\sim||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}v_{h}||_{L^{2}(T)}\sim||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}^{(1)}}\tilde{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\tilde{T})},

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{j}v_{h}|_{L^{2}(T)}\leq c\sum_{k=1}^{j}

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\Gamma}v_{h}^{\ell}|_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})},\qquad|\nabla_{\Gamma}^{j}v_{h}^{\ell}|_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}\leq c\sum_{k=1}^{j}|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}v_{h}|_{L^{2}(T^{\ell})}.

Proof.

The first two set of equivalences can be found in several literature, e.g.
[
15
,
11
]
. Regarding the last two inequalities, see
[
34
, Lemma 3.3]
.
∎

4.3.
Geometric perturbations

Analogously to the continuous case of Definition
2.2
we define similarly for the discrete case the following operators for
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}\mathbf{v}_{h}

\displaystyle:={\mathbf{P}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}

\displaystyle E_{h}(\mathbf{v}_{h})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}\mathbf{v}_{h}+\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov,t}\mathbf{v}_{h}),

Lemma 4.4
.

For
h
small enough,
\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
,
{\bm{f}}\in(L^{2}(\Gamma))^{3}
, we have the following perturbation bounds

(4.21)

\displaystyle|({\bm{f}},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell})_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}-({\bm{f}}^{-\ell},\mathbf{v}_{h})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}||{\bm{f}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})},

(4.22)

\displaystyle||(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell})^{-\ell}-\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},

(4.23)

\displaystyle|(E({\mathbf{w}}_{h}^{\ell}),E(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}))_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}-(E_{h}({\mathbf{w}}_{h}),E_{h}(\mathbf{v}_{h}))_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}}||{\mathbf{w}}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},

(4.24)

\displaystyle||E({\mathbf{w}}_{h}^{\ell})^{-\ell}-E_{h}({\mathbf{w}}_{h})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}}||{\mathbf{w}}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}.

Proof.

Refer to
[
30
,
15
]
for the initial two estimates, which can be easily generalized to vector-valued functions, and
[
17
, Lemma 4.6]
for the remaining two.
∎

4.4.
Interpolation

4.4.1.
Lagrange Interpolation

We first introduce the standard Lagrange-type interpolant
\widetilde{I}_{h}:(C^{0}(\Gamma_{h}))^{3}\rightarrow(S^{k}_{h,k_{g}})^{3}
, which approximates sufficiently smooth
vector-valued
functions and its lift
{I}_{h}{\bm{\eta}}=(\widetilde{I}_{h}{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell})^{\ell}
on the smooth surface
\Gamma
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Given
{\bm{\eta}}\in\bm{H}^{k+1}(\Gamma)
the Lagrangian interpolant satisfies the following bounds

(4.25)

||{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell}-\widetilde{I}_{h}{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+h||{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell}-\widetilde{I}_{h}{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k+1}||{\bm{\eta}}||_{H^{k+1}(\Gamma)}.

for some integer
k
, with lift
{I}_{h}({\bm{\eta}})=\widetilde{I}_{h}({\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell})^{\ell}
.
Furthermore, if
{\bm{\eta}}\in(W^{k+1}_{\infty}(\Gamma))^{3}
then it also holds that

(4.26)

||{\bm{\eta}}-{I}_{h}{\bm{\eta}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}+h||\nabla_{\Gamma}({\bm{\eta}}-{I}_{h}{\bm{\eta}})||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq ch^{k+1}||{\bm{\eta}}||_{W^{k+1}_{\infty}(\Gamma)}.

Proof.

These bounds are just extensions of the scalar bounds. The first estimate (
4.25
) can be derived from standard Lagrange interpolation theory
[
15
,
11
]
. The last estimate (
4.26
)
can be obtained as in
[
18
, Lemma 6.24]
or
[
5
, Theorem 4.5.11]
.
∎

4.4.2.
Scott-Zhang Interpolation

For the stability analysis we will need to make use of the Scott-Zhang interpolant, and specifically its projection and super-approximation properties.

Let us define the Scott-Zhang interpolant
\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}:(L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}))^{n}\rightarrow(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k})^{n}
for
n=1,3
. Then, for each element
T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
and
{\mathbf{v}}\in(L^{2}(\Gamma))^{n}
the following estimate holds

(4.27)

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{v}}^{-\ell}-\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}{\mathbf{v}}^{-\ell}||_{H^{l}(T)}\leq ch^{l-k+1}||{\mathbf{v}}||_{H^{k+1}(\omega_{\tilde{T}})}

where
\omega_{\tilde{T}}
is a union of neighbouring elements of the element
T
. The following stability bound also holds

(4.28)

||\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}{\mathbf{v}}^{-\ell}||_{H^{1}(T)}\leq c||{\mathbf{v}}^{-\ell}||_{H^{1}(\omega_{\tilde{T}})}\leq c||{\mathbf{v}}||_{H^{1}(\omega_{\tilde{T}}^{\ell})}.

We omit further details, but if one requires more information about the construction of the Scott-Zhang interpolant on high-order surfaces, we refer to
[
22
,
17
]
.

Finally, we present a super-approximation type of estimate and stability for the Scott-Zhang interpolant. This type of estimate has been proven in
[
22
]
due to the projection property of
\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}
onto the finite element space
(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k})^{3}
.
We present the lemma for completeness.

Lemma 4.5
.

For discrete functions
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k})^{3}
and
\chi\in[W_{\infty}^{3}(\Gamma)]^{3}
it holds

(4.29)

\displaystyle\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}(I-\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ})(\chi^{-\ell}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h})||_{L^{2}(T)}\leq ch||\chi||_{W_{\infty}^{3}(\Gamma)}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},

(4.30)

\displaystyle\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}||(I-\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ})(\chi^{-\ell}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h})||_{L^{2}(T)}\leq ch||\chi||_{W_{\infty}^{3}(\Gamma)}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}.

We also have the
L^{2}
stability estimate

(4.31)

\displaystyle||\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}(\chi^{-\ell}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq c||\chi^{-\ell}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}.

5.
The fully Discrete scheme

As in the Stokes case
[
17
]
, we consider the
Taylor-Hood
surface finite elements
\mathrm{\mathbf{P}}_{k_{u}}
/
\mathrm{P}_{k_{pr}}
/
\mathrm{P}_{k_{\lambda}}

for a
k_{g}
-order approximation of the surface, (c.f. Section
4.2
), and set

k_{u}\geq 2,~k_{pr}=k_{u}-1~~\mbox{and}~~k_{\lambda}\geq 1.

We maintain the notation
k_{g},k_{u},k_{pr}
and
k_{\lambda}
in order to track how error dependencies arise and define the following finite element spaces

(5.1)

V_{h}=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{u}},~~{\mathbf{V}}_{h}=(S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{u}})^{3},~~Q_{h}=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{pr}}\cap L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma_{h}),~~\Lambda_{h}=S_{h,k_{g}}^{k_{\lambda}}.

In particular, the eventual choice of
k_{\lambda}
plays an important role in the stability and convergence of the two discrete pressures
p_{h},\lambda_{h}
. We will see that for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}
we obtain optimal convergence rates using
iso-parametric surface finite elements
, while for
k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1
we lose an order of convergence with respect to the geometric approximation of the surface, and therefore to obtain the same convergence one has to use
super-parametric surface finite elements
. This choice, as we notice in the numerical results section
Section

8
, is also important for the underlined conditioning of the system.

We define the space of
discrete weakly tangential divergence-free functions

(5.2)

{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}:=\{\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}:b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{w}_{h},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})=0,~~\forall\,\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\}\in Q_{h}\times\Lambda_{h}\}.

Finally, we will need the dual space of the finite element space
\Lambda_{h}
, which we denote as

H_{h}^{-1}(\Gamma_{h})
. For
\ell_{h}\in\Lambda_{h}
we define its dual norm as

(5.3)

||\ell_{h}||_{H_{h}^{-1}(\Gamma_{h})}=\sup_{\xi_{h}\in\Lambda_{h}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{<\ell_{h},\xi_{h}>_{H^{-1},H^{1}}}{||\xi_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}}=\sup_{\xi_{h}\in\Lambda_{h}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{(\ell_{h},\xi_{h})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}}{||\xi_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}}.

The layout of the rest of the section is as follows. We define our bilinear forms in
Section

5.1
and prove some important properties in
Section

5.2
. Then, in
Section

5.3
we recall some perturbation bounds and the interpolation estimates in
||\cdot||_{a_{h}}
-norm. In
Sections

5.4
and
5.5
we define new surface Ritz-Stokes and Leray projections. Finally, in
Section

5.6
, we define our fully discrete scheme for the time-dependent Navier-Stokes.

5.1.
The discrete bilinear forms

The finite element method relies on the following discrete bilinear forms and inertia terms: For
{\mathbf{z}}_{h},\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}\subset\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
,
\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\}\in Q_{h}\times\Lambda_{h}
we define

(5.4)

\displaystyle a_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma_{h}}E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h}):E_{h}(\mathbf{v}_{h})d\sigma_{h}+\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}d\sigma_{h},

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{h}({\mathbf{z}}_{h};\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Gamma_{h}}(({\mathbf{z}}_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov})\mathbf{w}_{h})\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma_{h}}(({\mathbf{z}}_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov})\mathbf{v}_{h})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}\Big)

(5.5)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int_{\Gamma_{h}}(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})\mathbf{z}_{h}\cdot\bm{H}_{h}\mathbf{v}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma_{h}}(\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})\mathbf{z}_{h}\cdot\bm{H}_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}\;d\sigma_{h}\Big),

(5.6)

\displaystyle b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{w}_{h},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}q_{h}\,d\sigma_{h}+\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\xi_{h}\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\,d\sigma_{h},

(5.7)

\displaystyle b_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h},q_{h})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}q_{h}\,d\sigma_{h}.

Remark 5.1
.

The main reason for using (
5.1
) to approximate the trilinear inertia term is to provide a skew-symmetrized discrete analogue to (
7.6
), since we use
H^{1}
-conforming Taylor-Hood elements; as seen in the literature e.g.
[
45
]
. In the discrete setting, recalling (
2.9
), one can replace
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}
by
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}\mathbf{w}_{h}-(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})\bm{H}_{h}
, giving rise to (
5.1
).

In our stability analysis we use the energy norm

(5.8)

||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}=||E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})},

for a velocity finite element vector
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
. We also readily see that

(5.9)

||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}\leq||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}.

5.2.
Properties of the discrete bilinear forms

We present some known uniform interpolation and Korn-type inequalities that can be found in
[
17
,
28
]
. We also extend them using Broken Sobolev spaces.

Lemma 5.1

(Interpolation and Korn-type inequalities)
.

The following important uniform inequalities hold

(5.10)

\displaystyle||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq c(||E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+h^{-1}||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})})\ \ \textnormal{ for all }\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h},

(5.11)

\displaystyle||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{-2}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}^{2}\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \textnormal{ for all }\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h},

(5.12)

\displaystyle||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{4}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq c||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}^{1/2}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}^{1/2}\quad\quad\textnormal{ for all }\mathbf{w}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h}).

Proof.

Estimates (
5.10
), (
5.11
) follow from
[
17
, Lemma 4.7]
, while (
5.12
) holds due to
[
36
, Lemma 4.2]
.
∎

Now we prove a special case regarding the discrete Korn-type inequality, with the help of the broken Sobolev spaces. The arguments follow similar to
[
23
]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
we have

(5.13)

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq c\,a_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{w}_{h}),

(5.14)

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq c||\mathbf{w}_{h}||^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}^{1/2}\leq c||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}.

Proof.

For the first estimate we consider two cases. First for
k_{g}=1
we have that
{\mathbf{n}}_{h}
, and therefore
{\mathbf{P}_{h}}
, is constant on each triangle giving rise to the expression
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}({\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h})={\mathbf{P}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}\mathbf{w}_{h}
, which leads to our desired result,

||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}\leq||\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}.

Now for
k_{g}\geq 2
we split our expression as followed

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}\leq||({\mathbf{P}_{h}}-{\mathbf{P}})\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}+||{\mathbf{P}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}.

For the first term we use
[
23
, Lemma 4.4]
to get

||({\mathbf{P}_{h}}-{\mathbf{P}})\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}\leq h^{k_{g}-1}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})},

while the second term following
[
17
, Lemma 4.7]
, applying Korn’s inequality (
3.4
), and
Lemma

4.4
gives

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{P}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq c||{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}\leq c||E({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+c||{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq c\big(||E(\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+||(\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})\bm{H}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+||{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq c||E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+c||(E(\mathbf{w}_{h}^{\ell}))^{-\ell}-E_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+c||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq c||E_{h}(\mathbf{v}_{h})||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+c(1+h^{k_{g}-1})||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{(by inv. ineq.)}.

Combining these results (
5.13
) follows. As for (
5.14
) applying (
5.13
) on (
5.12
) yields the required estimate.
∎

Now, the following continuity and coercivity estimates for the bilinear and trilinear forms hold.

Lemma 5.3

(Bounds and coercivity)
.

The following bounds and coercivity estimates hold:

(5.15)

\displaystyle a_{h}(\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h})

\displaystyle\leq||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{a_{h}},

(5.16)

\displaystyle b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{w}_{h},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})

\displaystyle\leq c||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}||q_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\xi_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}||\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})},

(5.17)

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{h}(\mathbf{z}_{h};\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h})

\displaystyle\leq c||\mathbf{z}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}^{1/2}||\mathbf{z}_{h}||^{1/2}_{a_{h}}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||^{1/2}_{a_{h}}

\displaystyle\quad+c||\mathbf{z}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}^{1/2}||\mathbf{z}_{h}||^{1/2}_{a_{h}}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{a_{h}}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}^{1/2}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||^{1/2}_{a_{h}},

\textnormal{ for all }\mathbf{z}_{h},\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
, and
(q_{h},\xi_{h})\in Q_{h}\times\Lambda_{h}
.

Proof.

The first estimate can be readily seen by a simple use of Schwarz’s inequality. To see (
5.16
) we calculate with the help of the integration by parts formula (
5.25
) that,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}q_{h}\ d\sigma_{h}

\displaystyle\leq c||q_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}+ch^{k_{g}-1}||q_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq c||q_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}},

where in the first inequality we used the
|[{\mathbf{m}}_{h}]|\leq ch^{k_{g}}
bound for the co-normal (
4.5
) (cf.
[
18
]
) and the inequalities
||q_{h}||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{h})}\leq ch^{-1/2}||q_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}
, and
||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{h})}\leq ch^{-1/2}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}
. Regarding the last inequality, from (
5.1
) and simple Cauchy-Schwarz bounds, we readily see that

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{h}(\mathbf{z}_{h};\mathbf{w}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h})

\displaystyle\leq c||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{z}_{h}||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{a_{h}}||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}+c||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{z}_{h}||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{a_{h}}||{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{T}_{h})}.

The final result is readily seen with the help of the previously proven results in
Lemma

5.2
.
∎

Now, let us prove some important properties that involve the
discrete weakly tangential divergence-free functions

\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
(
5.2
). These properties will be used throughout the rest of text.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
and
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
, then we have the following inequalties

(5.18)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})},\ \text{ for }k_{\lambda}=k_{u}.

(5.19)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},\ \text{ for }k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1.

Proof.

In the case where
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
, we have that the finite element spaces
\Lambda_{h}=S^{k_{\lambda}}_{h,k_{g}}=S^{k_{u}}_{h,k_{g}}=V_{h}
, therefore, since
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
, cf. (
5.2
), we see that
\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\xi_{h}({\mathbf{w}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})=0
for any
\xi_{h}\in V_{h}
. So, using the estimate
||{\mathbf{n}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}}\leq ch^{k_{g}}
; see (
4.5
), the following computations follow

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot({\mathbf{n}}_{h}-{\mathbf{n}}))_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}-\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}(\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}))_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}}_{(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}(\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}))_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}=0,\ \widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}(\cdot)\in V_{h}=\Lambda_{h}}+ch^{k_{g}}||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

(5.20)

\displaystyle\leq ch||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})},

where in the last inequality we used the super-approximation estimate for the Scott-Zhang interpolant (
4.30
), which holds since
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
and
\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}(\cdot):L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})\rightarrow V_{h}
.

On the other hand, in the case where
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
we see that
\Lambda_{h}\neq V_{h}
, i.e. now
\Lambda_{h}
is not rich enough, and therefore
\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\xi_{h}({\mathbf{w}}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})=0
only for
\xi_{h}\in\Lambda_{h}
. For that reason, in the above eq. (
5.2
) we, instead, restrict ourselves to Scott-Zhang interpolants projected to
\Lambda_{h}
, that is,
(\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ})^{\lambda}(\cdot):L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})\rightarrow\Lambda_{h}
. That means the super-approximation estimate (
4.30
) does not hold (since
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
), and therefore we use the standard interpolation estimate (
4.27
) to obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}-(\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ})^{\lambda}(\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}))_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}}_{(\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h},(\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ})^{\lambda}(\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}))_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}=0,\ \widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}(\cdot)\in\Lambda_{h}}+ch^{k_{g}}||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}.

∎

The following results are an immediate consequence of the above
Lemma

5.4
.

Corollary 5.4.1
.

Let
\mathbf{w}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
, then if
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}-1}
we have the following

(5.21)

\displaystyle||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}.

Furthermore, if
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
we have, for
h\leq h_{0}
with sufficiently small
h_{0}

(5.22)

\displaystyle||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})},

(5.23)

\displaystyle||\mathbf{w}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}

\displaystyle\leq ch||{\mathbf{P}}\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+ch^{k_{g}+1}||\mathbf{w}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}.

Lemma 5.5

(Improved
H^{1}
coercivity bound)
.

If
\underline{k_{\lambda}=k_{u}}
and
{\mathbf{w}}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
then the following holds

(5.24)

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{w}}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq ca_{h}({\mathbf{w}}_{h},{\mathbf{w}}_{h}).

Proof.

This can be immediately proven by combining the discrete Korn’s inequality (
5.10
) and (
5.22
).
∎

We also have the following integration by parts formula.

Lemma 5.6
.

On the discrete surface
\Gamma_{h}
the following integration by parts formula holds:

(5.25)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}q_{h}\ d\sigma_{h}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Gamma_{h}}q_{h}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma_{h}}\mathbf{v}_{h}\ d\sigma_{h}+\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\int_{T}(\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h})q_{h}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma_{h}}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}\ d\sigma_{h}

\displaystyle+\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{h}}\int_{E}[{\mathbf{m}}_{h}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{h}]q_{h}\,d\ell,

for
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
and
q_{h}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
, for any
k,l\geq\in\mathbb{N}
.

5.3.
Perturbation bounds and interpolation bounds in energy norm

Recalling
Section

4.3
we present geometric perturbations for the bilinear forms; see
[
17
]
for more details.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
{\mathbf{w}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
and
\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})\times H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
. Then we have

(5.26)

\displaystyle|a({\mathbf{w}}_{h}^{\ell},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell})-a_{h}({\mathbf{w}}_{h},\mathbf{v}_{h})|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}}||{\mathbf{w}}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},

\displaystyle|b_{h}^{L}({\mathbf{w}}_{h},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})-b^{L}({\mathbf{w}}_{h}^{\ell},\{q_{h}^{\ell},\xi_{h}^{\ell}\})|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}}||{\mathbf{w}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}(||q_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}+||\xi_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})})

(5.27)

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}-1}||{\mathbf{w}}_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}(||q_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}+h||\xi_{h}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}).

If
{\mathbf{w}}\in\bm{H}_{T}^{1}
, then we get the following higher order bound

(5.28)

\displaystyle|b_{h}^{L}({\mathbf{w}}^{-\ell},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})-b^{L}({\mathbf{w}},\{q_{h}^{\ell},\xi_{h}^{\ell}\})|\leq ch^{k_{g}}||{\mathbf{w}}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}||\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}.

Furthermore if
{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}}\in\bm{H}_{T}^{1}\cap\bm{H}^{2}(\Gamma)
then we have

(5.29)

\displaystyle|b_{h}^{L}({\mathbf{w}}^{-\ell},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})-b^{L}({\mathbf{w}},\{q_{h}^{\ell},\xi_{h}^{\ell}\})|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}||{\mathbf{w}}||_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}||\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},

(5.30)

\displaystyle|a({\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{v}})-a_{h}({\mathbf{w}}^{-\ell},{\mathbf{v}}^{-\ell})|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}||{\mathbf{w}}||_{H^{2}(\Gamma)}||{\mathbf{v}}||_{H^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}.

Proof.

The first estimate can be easily proved with the help of (
4.23
). For the rest (
5.7
) - (
5.29
), see
[
17
, Lemma 6.1]
. The last estimate can be proved as in
[
22
, Lemma 5.4]
with suitable alterations.
∎

Lemma 5.8

(Weingarten map consistency bound)
.

The following bound holds for all
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{h})
,

(5.31)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\beta^{-\ell}\,\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot\bm{H}_{h}{\mathbf{u}}^{-\ell}\,d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\bm{H}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big|\leq ch^{k_{g}}||\beta||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}.

Proof.

This proof follows similarly to
[
36
, Appendix C]
. Despite that, we present the proof for clarity reasons. First, recall that
\bm{H}_{h}=\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}={\mathbf{P}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}
and by (
4.5
) we know that
||\bm{H}-\bm{H}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}-1}
. The idea is that with the help of the product rule
\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{w}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}})^{t}={\mathbf{w}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}}+{\mathbf{n}}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{w}})
and integration by parts (
2.12
) we could gain one approximation order by shifting the derivative to the test function instead.

First, let us add and subtract an appropriate term, the reason for which we will discuss just right after:

(5.32)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\beta^{-\ell}\,\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot\bm{H}_{h}{\mathbf{u}}^{-\ell}\,d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\bm{H}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\beta^{-\ell}\,\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot\bm{H}_{h}{\mathbf{u}}^{-\ell}\,d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big|+\Big|\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\bm{H}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big|.

Recall that
\bm{H}=\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}}={\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla{\mathbf{n}}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}
. By lifting; see
Section

4.2.4
, we get
\bm{H}_{h}={\mathbf{P}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{B}}_{h}^{t}
. With that in mind, along with the estimates
||{\mathbf{P}}-{\mathbf{P}_{h}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}}
, cf. (
4.6
),
||1-\mu_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}+1}
, cf. (
4.8
), and
||{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}-{\bm{B}_{h}}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq ch^{k_{g}}
cf. (
4.18
), and the embedding
L^{4}\hookrightarrow H^{1}
, we clearly see that

(5.33)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Gamma_{h}}\beta^{-\ell}\,\mathbf{v}_{h}\cdot\bm{H}_{h}{\mathbf{u}}^{-\ell}\,d\sigma_{h}-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{\mu_{h}}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot{\mathbf{P}_{h}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{B}}_{h}^{t}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big|

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}}||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||\beta||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},

where we also also used the norm equivalence (
4.19
). For the second absolute value term in (
5.32
), using the product rule we see that

(5.34)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\bm{H}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{n}}){\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma=\underbrace{\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot{\mathbf{n}})^{t}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma}_{=0}-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,({\mathbf{n}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}){\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma.

Furthermore, using partial integration
\int_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}q=-\int_{\Gamma}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}\,q+\int_{\Gamma}tr(\bm{H})\,q\,({\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}})
, we obtain the following, due to the fact that
{\mathbf{u}}\cdot{\mathbf{n}_{\Gamma}}=0
,

(5.35)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}({\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell})^{t}\beta{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,({\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}){\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Gamma}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\beta{\mathbf{u}}){\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}\,d\sigma-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,({\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}){\mathbf{u}}.

Combining now (
5.34
), (
5.35
), along with the fact that
||{\mathbf{n}}-{\mathbf{n}}_{h}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq ch^{k_{g}}
, and
||{\mathbf{P}_{\Gamma}}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}||_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq ch^{k_{g}}
; see (
4.5
), (
4.6
), it follows that

(5.36)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}^{cov}{\mathbf{n}}_{h}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma-\int_{\Gamma}\beta\,\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}\cdot\bm{H}{\mathbf{u}}\,d\sigma\Big|\leq ch^{k_{g}}||\beta||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})},

where in (
5.35
) we also used the fact that
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\beta{\mathbf{u}})=\beta\,\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}{\mathbf{u}}+{\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\beta
and the inclusion
L^{4}\hookrightarrow H^{1}
. Inserting the results (
5.33
), (
5.36
) into (
5.32
) gives our desired estimates.
∎

Lemma 5.9

(Scott-Zhang interpolant in energy norm)
.

Let
\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}(\cdot):(L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}))^{3}\rightarrow{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
be the Scott-Zhang interpolant onto the finite element
{\mathbf{V}}_{h}
, then for
{\bm{\eta}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
the following interpolation estimates hold in the discrete energy norm,

(5.37)

||{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell}-\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell}||_{a_{h}}\leq ch^{k_{u}}||{\bm{\eta}}||_{H^{k_{u}+1}(\Gamma)}.

Furthermore, if
{\bm{\eta}}\in\bm{H}^{1}_{T}(\Gamma)
and
k_{g}\geq 2
, then we have the stability bound

(5.38)

||\widetilde{I}_{h}^{SZ}({\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell})||_{a_{h}}\leq||{\bm{\eta}}||_{a}\leq||{\bm{\eta}}^{-\ell}||_{a_{h}},

where the constants
c
are independent of the mesh parameter
h
and time
t
.

Proof.

The first estimate is derived from the bound (
4.27
), and the fact that
||\cdot||_{a_{h}}\leq c||\cdot||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}
(
5.9
). The stability bound follows
from the Scott-Zhang stability (
4.28
) and Korn’s inequality (
3.4
).

∎

5.4.
Surface Ritz-Stokes projection

Ritz maps have been studied in the case of elliptic operators on stationary and evolving surfaces
[
16
,
31
]
. On the other hand, Ritz-Stokes maps have been studied for stationary domains in the case of no geometric variational crimes, that is,
\Omega_{h}=\Omega
; see
[
2
,
10
,
6
]
. In this work, we extend these results to the case of discrete surfaces, where
\Gamma_{h}\neq\Gamma
. We will consider a standard Ritz-Stokes projection and a modified Ritz-Stokes projection similar to the one introduced for the stationary domain case in
[
2
,
10
]
.

For convenience, from now on we shall sometimes omit the inverse lift extension
(\cdot)^{-\ell}
notation, unless specified, since it should be clear from the context if e.g.
a function
{\mathbf{u}}
is defined on
\Gamma
or
\Gamma_{h}
.

Definition 5.2

(Modified Ritz-Stokes projection)
.

Given
({\mathbf{u}},\{p,\lambda\})\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\times(L^{2}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma))
we define by
\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
,
\{\mathcal{P}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathcal{L}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})\}\in Q_{h}\times\Lambda_{h}
the unique projection such that

(5.39)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}a_{h}(\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathbf{v}_{h})+b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h},\{\mathcal{P}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathcal{L}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})\})=a({\mathbf{u}},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell}),\\
b_{h}^{L}(\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})=0,\end{cases}

for all
(\mathbf{v}_{h},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}\times(Q_{h}\times\Lambda_{h})
. The lift is then defined typically
\big(\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\ell}({\mathbf{u}})
,
\{\mathcal{P}_{h}^{\ell}({\mathbf{u}}),\,\mathcal{L}_{h}^{\ell}({\mathbf{u}})\}\big)\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{\ell}\times(Q_{h}^{\ell}\times\Lambda_{h}^{\ell})
.

Definition 5.3

(Ritz-Stokes projection)
.

Given
({\mathbf{u}},\{p,\lambda\})\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\times(L^{2}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma))
we define by
\mathcal{R}_{h}^{b}({\mathbf{u}})=\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}},\{p,\lambda\})\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
,
\mathcal{P}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})=\mathcal{P}_{h}({\mathbf{u}},\{p,\lambda\})\in Q_{h},\,\mathcal{L}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})=\mathcal{L}_{h}({\mathbf{u}},\{p,\lambda\})\in\Lambda_{h}
the unique projection such that

(5.40)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}a_{h}(\mathcal{R}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathbf{v}_{h})+b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h},\{\mathcal{P}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathcal{L}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})\})=a({\mathbf{u}},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell})+b^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell},\{p,\lambda\}),\\
b_{h}^{L}(\mathcal{R}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})=0,\end{cases}

for all
(\mathbf{v}_{h},\{q_{h},\xi_{h}\})\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}\times(Q_{h}\times\Lambda_{h})
. The lift is defined in the standard way.

We notice due to the well-posedness and stability of the surface Stokes equations
[
17
, Theorem 5.4]
, we have the following
a-priori
stability estimates for the Ritz-Stokes projections
\mathcal{R}_{h}^{(\cdot)}({\mathbf{u}})

\big(:=\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})
or
\mathcal{R}_{h}^{b}({\mathbf{u}})\big)

(5.41)

\displaystyle||\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})||_{a_{h}}^{2}+||\{\mathcal{P}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathcal{L}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}^{2}\leq c||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}^{2},

\displaystyle||\mathcal{R}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})||_{a_{h}}^{2}+||\{\mathcal{P}_{h}^{b}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathcal{L}^{b}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}^{2}\leq c\big(||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}^{2}+||\{p,\lambda\}||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\big).

Lemma 5.10

(pseudo Galerkin orthogonality)
.

For every
{\mathbf{u}}\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
it holds that

(5.42)

\displaystyle a_{h}(({\mathbf{u}}-\mathcal{R}_{h}^{(\cdot)}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathbf{v}_{h})\leq C^{(\cdot)}h^{k_{g}}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}\quad\text{ for all }\mathbf{v}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div},

for each projection, where the constant
C=c||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}
or
C^{b}=c(||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}+||p||_{H^{1}}+||\lambda||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)})
.

Proof.

Let us consider (
5.40
) since the Galerkin orthogonality for
\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}})
follows similarly. Using the perturbation estimates (
5.26
), (
5.28
) and the norm equivalence (
4.19
) we get for any
\mathbf{v}_{h}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{h}^{div}
that

\displaystyle a_{h}(({\mathbf{u}}-\mathcal{R}_{h}({\mathbf{u}}),\mathbf{v}_{h})

\displaystyle=a_{h}({\mathbf{u}},\mathbf{v}_{h})-a({\mathbf{u}},\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell})+b_{h}^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h},\{p,\lambda\})-b^{L}(\mathbf{v}_{h}^{\ell},\{p,\lambda\})

\displaystyle\leq ch^{k_{g}}||{\mathbf{u}}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})}+ch^{k_{g}}(||p||_{H^{1}}+||\lambda||_{L^{2}(\Gamma)})||\mathbf{v}_{h}||_{a_{h}},

which gives the desired result.
∎

Lemma 5.11

(Error Bounds modified Ritz-Stokes)
.

Let
({\mathbf{u}},\{p,\lambda\})\in\bm{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\times(H^{1}(\Gamma)\times L^{2}(\Gamma))
the solution of (
3.6
). Then, we have the following error bounds for the Ritz-Stokes projection for
a=0,1

(5.43)
